- **18.222** — ὄπα χάλκεον: Zenodotus read χαλκέην, which Aristonicus called unmetrical; Leaf (on 18.222) thinks it cannot be so dismissed, with forms like χρυσέην scanned by synizesis. χάλκεον, "bronze", is unwearied and perhaps ringing, after the trumpet (Leaf).
- **18.240** — ἀέκοντα: the sun goes down unwilling, because before his time; the only other interference of a god with the course of nature in Homer is Athena's holding back of the dawn (Od. 23.243; Leaf on 18.240).
- **18.250** — πρόσσω καὶ ὀπίσσω, "before and after": commonly taken of past and future; Leaf (on 18.250) doubts it, since πρόσσω is never temporal in Homer and ὀπίσσω, when temporal, always looks to the future, and takes the words of a man who looks about him on every side (cf. 1.343, 3.109; ἀμφὶ μάλα φράζεσθε, 18.254). The English leaves both open.
- **18.259** — χαίρεσκον is iterative and ἔγωγε emphatic, "for I myself used to rejoice": Leaf (on 18.259) refers it to the Trojans' bivouac on the plain after their success in Book 8, "I, the same who used to delight … am now afraid".
- **18.266–283** — Heyne, Düntzer, Nauck and Fick reject these lines as a later interpolation (two neglects of the digamma, several strange expressions); Leaf (on 18.266) would at most remove 272–276, since Hector's reply echoes Polydamas' words.
- **18.272** — ἀπʼ οὔατος, "far from my hearing" (again 22.454): perhaps a formula for unsaying an ill-omened word (Leaf on 18.272).
- **18.274** — σθένος ἕξομεν, "a very obscure phrase" (Leaf on 18.274): Aristarchus took it "we shall prevail by counsel"; it is commonly taken "we will keep our force in the place of assembly"; Leaf prefers "husband our strength by resting there". The English, "we will keep our strength in the place of assembly", follows him.
- **18.288–292** — Leaf (on 18.288) finds these lines confusing and without bearing on the situation: the loss of the city's wealth would argue for defense rather than attack, and νῦν δέ (290, 293) serves two different antitheses. περνάμενα (292), "sold away", perhaps to pay the allies (Leaf on 18.292; cf. 17.225).
- **18.300–302** — "very obscure in thought and expression" (Leaf on 18.300): apparently irony at Polydamas, that the man anxious for his possessions should give them to the people to consume in common rather than leave them to the Achaeans. καταδημοβορῆσαι (only here) is to consume from the common stock (Leaf on 18.301; cf. δημοβόρος 1.231). In 302 τῶν τινά is rendered "someone among the people", which suits τῶν of the people or of the possessions.
- **18.308** — ἤ κε φέρῃσι … ἦ κε φεροίμην, subjunctive then optative: Leaf (on 18.308) takes the optative as potential, the second alternative put hesitantly, "or I might" (contrast 13.486, where both are optative); one manuscript has φέροιτο, adopted by Nauck and von Christ, and Naber conjectured φέρωμι. ἦ κε is Allen's accentuation of the second member (as 9.619, 10.445).
- **18.309** — ξυνὸς Ἐνυάλιος: "the god of battle is impartial and slays him that would slay" (Leaf on 18.309, comparing 6.339); κτανέοντα may be desiderative, and Cobet would read the future κτενέοντα. Aristotle quotes the first words as a maxim (*Rhetoric* 1395a15).
- **18.316** — ἁδινοῦ, "vehement" (Leaf on 18.316, with 18.124 and 2.87), is "unceasing" with lamentation and groaning. The line recurs at 23.17.
- **18.319** — ἐλαφηβόλος "deer hunter": the poets use the word loosely of any hunter, as Aristonicus notes (Leaf on 18.319).
- **18.329** — ἐρεῦσαι: some scholars adopt the variant ἐρεύσειν, a future that Leaf (on 18.329) finds indefensible.
- **18.336–342** — 336–337 = 23.22–23. The lines may belong to both places, the later fulfilling the promise (23.175–176), or 336–342 may have been put here to prepare for Book 23 (Leaf on 18.336).
- **18.351** — ἐννεώροιο, "nine years old": Leaf (on 18.351) would write ἐννώροιο and takes the word as a round number for full maturity, oil being the better for keeping.
- **18.357** — ἔπρηξας, "you have had your way", only here without an object; for καί after ἔπειτα Bentley conjectured δή, and Leaf (on 18.357) explains καί as "once more", an allusion to Hera's earlier offenses.
- **18.370** — ἀστερόεντα, "starry": probably adorned with starlike ornaments (Leaf on 18.370, as 16.134).
- **18.376** — θεῖον ἀγῶνα, "the divine assembly" (7.298); nearly all manuscripts have the un-Homeric δύσονται, probably from 7.298 (Leaf on 18.376).
- **18.382** — Charis as the wife of Hephaestus is "a more transparent allegory than we find elsewhere in Homer", grace attending on craft (Leaf on 18.382, quoting the scholiast); in the lay of Demodocus (Od. 8) his wife is Aphrodite.
- **18.395–405** — Hephaestus thrown from heaven by his mother for his lameness: the fall at 1.590–594, where Zeus throws him out, has the same application to him with different details (Leaf on 18.395).
- **18.399** — Leaf (on 18.399) thinks Payne Knight right to expel the line as a gloss, since the repetition of the first of two names is very unusual. ἀψορρόου (again only Od. 20.65): the ancients explained it as flowing back into himself, since Oceanus encircles the earth; others have seen in it a vague report of the tide.
- **18.401** — The jewelry: πόρπαι are probably brooches or buckles; the rest of the line is copied in the *Hymn to Aphrodite* (86–89), whose author may not have known its sense either (Leaf on 18.401). "brooches and curved spirals, rosettes and necklaces" renders the usual guesses.
- **18.407** — ζῳάγρια, "the price for my life": first the price paid to a warrior who took a prisoner alive (cf. 6.46), here and at Od. 8.462 a playful extension (Leaf on 18.407). Nauck read θεᾷ for Θέτι.
- **18.410** — αἴητον, "a word of unknown meaning found only here in Greek", perhaps akin to ἄητον (21.395); πέλωρ is an odd word for Hephaestus (Leaf on 18.410). "panting" is a guess.
- **18.421** — ἔρρων: not simply "going" but going painfully because of his lameness, as Aristonicus explains it (Leaf on 18.421); hence "hobbling", kept apart from χωλεύων "limping" (18.411, 18.417).
- **18.453** — πᾶν ἦμαρ: the fighting at the Scaean gates was not "all day long" (Leaf on 18.453, as with πανημερίοις at 17.384), "but this is not the place where we need expect the accuracy of a chronicler".
- **18.458** — υἱεῖ ἐμῷ ὠκυμόρῳ: Aristarchus read υἱεῖ ἐμʼ ὠκυμόρῳ, a crasis without parallel in Homer; Leaf (on 18.458) would accept υἷʼ ἐμῷ.
- **18.466** — παρέσσεται: Zenodotus and Aristophanes read παρέξομαι; τις … ἀνθρώπων πολέων, "many a one of the many men there be" (Leaf on 18.466).
- **18.472** — ἄλλοτε δʼ αὖτε, "and vice versa": the blast was at hand when Hephaestus worked hard and ceased when he did not need it (Leaf on 18.472, comparing Od. 11.303); hence "and now otherwise".
- **18.480** — τρίπλαξ ἄντυξ: Löschcke took it as a threefold plaited ornament on the rim; Leaf (on 18.480) prefers a real part of the shield, τρίπλαξ being a form of τριπλός "threefold": perhaps three of the layers of hide turned up to form the rim, or three metal bands.
- **18.483–608** — Zenodotus rejected the whole description of the Shield (Leaf on 18.483). Agallis of Corcyra read it as the early history of Attica, the two cities being Attica and Eleusis, and a line added after 18.550 is hers (Leaf on 18.483, 18.551); Maximus of Tyre took the cities for Phaeacia and Ithaca.
- **18.485** — τείρεα, only here in Homer, "constellations", probably not from τέρας (Leaf on 18.485); ἐστεφάνωται, "with which the sky is crowned"; Zenodotus read ἐστήρικται, and Aristarchus is said to have read ἐστεφάνωκε, which Leaf doubts.
- **18.486–489** — The constellations, as Od. 5.272–275, where Boötes is named. The Pleiades and Hyades are often explained as "the sailing stars" and "the rainy stars"; Leaf (on 18.486) inclines to other derivations and notes that the older form of Orion's name is Ὠαρίων. The Bear alone has no share in the baths of Oceanus because the other circumpolar constellations were later inventions (Leaf on 18.489); Düntzer and Nauck read αἰεί, needlessly. Aristotle explains οἴη as a metaphor, the best known being called the only one (*Poetics* 1461a20–21).
- **18.492** — ἐκ θαλάμων: Zenodotus read ἐς θαλάμους, "not unconvincing" (Didymus); there is little to choose between them (Leaf on 18.492).
- **18.497–508** — The trial. "the one vowed to pay it all … the other refused to take anything" follows Leaf (on 18.500): μηδέν, only here in Homer, implies will, where a denial of fact would need οὐδέν; πιφαύσκων is "declaring", by speech, or "displaying" the full price. At 499 Zenodotus and most copies read ἀποκταμένου "slain", which brings out the homicide; the English renders the printed ἀποφθιμένου, "who had perished" (Leaf on 18.499). ἐπὶ ἴστορι (501), "at the hands of an arbiter", is a use of ἐπί without exact parallel in Homer, and πεῖραρ is "an issue, an end of the matter" (Leaf on 18.501). The Massaliot text read ἐπίπνυον, "incited", for ἐπήπυον, "shouted assent" (Leaf on 18.502). The elders sit in the sacred circle of polished stones (Leaf on 18.504) and "spring up" to speak, staff in hand; Döderlein referred ἤϊσσον to the litigants (Leaf on 18.506). The σκῆπτρα are plural loosely, handed in turn to each speaker (Leaf on 18.505).
- **18.505** — ἠεροφώνων: Döderlein derived it from ἀείρω, "lifting up the voice"; from ἀήρ it would be "misty-voiced", "from which no good sense can be got"; Ahrens conjectured ἱεροφώνων, "strong-voiced" (Leaf on 18.505). "far-voiced", a voice that carries, keeps it apart from λιγύφθογγοι and ἠπύτα.
- **18.511** — ἄνδιχα πάντα δάσασθαι: to divide the city's goods in two, half left to the citizens and half taken by the besiegers as ransom, the terms Hector thinks of offering Achilles (22.117–120; Leaf on 18.511).
- **18.513** — ὑπεθωρήσσοντο, "were secretly arming", a use of ὑπο- that does not recur in Homer; La Roche wrote λόχῳ δʼ ὑπὸ θωρήσσοντο (Leaf on 18.513).
- **18.519** — ὑπολίζονες, the printed reading, "smaller"; Leaf (on 18.519) prefers ὑπʼ ὀλίζονες, "smaller at their feet", calling the compound, "somewhat smaller", poetically inadmissible, though old (cf. ὑφήσσων in the *Shield of Heracles*, 258). The English "and the people were smaller" renders both.
- **18.523** — τοῖσι, "for them", the ambushers from the city (Leaf on 18.523); van Leeuwen took the ambush to be the besiegers', which Leaf rejects because of 530–532.
- **18.531** — εἰράων, only here (εἰρέας, *Theogony* 804): explained by the ancients as "places of assembly", from εἴρω "speak"; Leaf (on 18.531) finds the usual explanation unsatisfactory, since προπάροιθε calls for something narrower, but sees nothing better. Many manuscripts have ἰράων or ἱράων.
- **18.535–538** — These lines occur almost word for word in the *Shield of Heracles* (156–159), and have been thought copied from there; Leaf (on 18.535) finds them not alien to epic thought (4.440, 11.3). ὁμίλεον, which Leaf glosses "joined in the fray", is "were in the throng", keeping "the fray" for μόθος (18.537).
- **18.541–549** — The plowing: Leaf (on 18.541) suggests, after Ridgeway, the common field, plowed by the whole community on one day; the field is "fallow" (νειός) under a two-field system, τρίπολον "thrice plowed". Fick would cut 544–546 (Leaf on 18.545). At 548 ἀρηρομένῃ; the variant ἀρηρεμένῃ is "a mere blunder" (Leaf on 18.548).
- **18.550** — βασιλήϊον, the printed reading, "a king's domain"; most editors adopt the vulgate βαθυλήϊον, "deep with grain" (cf. *Shield of Heracles* 288; 11.560). Leaf (on 18.550) holds both possible and finds βασιλήϊον meaningful after the common field: the royal land, reaped by hired laborers.
- **18.564** — κυανέην, "of dark blue", names the material, kyanos, as κασσιτέρου after it shows (Leaf on 18.564).
- **18.570** — λίνον: Zenodotus read λίνος, the string of the lyre, and some took the vulgate λίνον so; Aristarchus took it as a kind of song, like παιήων, which is now generally accepted (Leaf on 18.570): "the Linus song", a dirge.
- **18.571–572** — ῥήσσοντες: beating time with their feet, or perhaps breaking into song (Leaf on 18.571). ἰυγμῷ, only here: "shouting", or a share in the Linus dirge (Leaf on 18.572).
- **18.575** — κόπρου, in a local sense, the byre (Leaf on 18.575; Od. 10.411): "out of the dung-yard".
- **18.576** — ῥοδανόν, so the manuscripts, not found again in Greek, perhaps the same as ῥαδινόν "pliant, waving"; Zenodotus read ῥαδαλόν, and one scholiast παρὰ Ῥοδανόν, a river (Leaf on 18.576). Hence "swaying reeds".
- **18.590–606** — The dance is Cretan in character (591–592; the scholia call the tumblers and the armed dance Cretan); some have concluded that 590–605 are a Cretan poet's interpolation, which Leaf (on 18.590) cannot refute but finds unproved. At 591 some ancient critics, offended that a god should copy a mortal's work, put a stop after 590 and read οἶον, "the only thing ever made like it" (Leaf on 18.591).
- **18.597–598** — Athetized, as Aristonicus reports, because Homer never calls a sword μάχαιρα and dancers should not carry knives; the lines were not in Aristophanes' text. Leaf (on 18.597) finds neither argument weighty.
- **18.604–606** — The printed text has the singer, μετὰ δέ σφιν ἐμέλπετο θεῖος ἀοιδὸς / φορμίζων (604–605), and keeps ἐξάρχοντες (606), so that the two tumblers lead the song and dance. Editors since Wolf inserted the words on the authority of Athenaeus (5.181c), who says that Aristarchus removed the singer; Leaf (on 18.604) thinks that evidence worthless and the words no part of any recognized text, and those who keep the singer change ἐξάρχοντες to ἐξάρχοντος. The words stand at Od. 4.17–19. The English renders the printed text.
- **18.610** — Leaf (on 18.610) reports Reichel's view that the line, which dismisses the corselet so briefly, is a later addition.
- **18.613** — "greaves of soft tin": the difficulty of so soft a metal, which ἑανοῦ ("pliant", Leaf) underlines, is removed by Reichel's view that the greaves guarded the shins against the edge of the shield, not against missiles (Leaf on 18.613). ἑανός keeps "soft", as at 5.734 and 18.352.
- **19.4** — περικείμενον with the dative, "lying with his arms around Patroclus", as ἀμφὶ … χυμένη "threw herself around him" (19.284; Od. 8.527). Heyne, Bentley and Brandreth altered the line to save the digamma of ὅν (Leaf on 19.4).
- **19.9** — ἐπεὶ δὴ πρῶτα, "since … once and for all": πρῶτα marks a starting point that is not to be gone behind (Leaf on 19.9, comparing 1.235).
- **19.12** — ὣς ἄρα φωνήσασα: the digitized text printed ὡς, the only ὡς ἄρα φων- in Homer (ὣς in the other sixty, and in this book at 19.37); a slip corrected here.
- **19.24–27** — ἄλκιμον υἱόν is governed in thought by ἀεικίσσωσι, the verb uppermost in Achilles' mind, though in strict grammar by καδδῦσαι; after the parenthesis ἐκ δʼ αἰὼν πέφαται ("the life is slain out of it": αἰών "life", as 5.685, 16.453, Od. 5.160) the construction returns to the subjunctive σαπήῃ, with χρόα the part affected (Leaf on 19.24, 19.27). Some scholia took αἰών for the spinal cord, a sense it has in Hippocrates; Leaf dismisses it.
- **19.32** — τελεσφόρον εἰς ἐνιαυτόν, "for a full year's round": the year that brings the seasons and the crops to completion, elsewhere only in the Odyssey; κεῖται is a subjunctive (Leaf on 19.32).
- **19.38–39** — The nectar and ambrosia dripped κατὰ ῥινῶν, "through his nostrils": Heyne saw a reminiscence of Egyptian embalming (Herodotus 2.86); Eustathius took ῥινῶν from ῥινός, "through the hides" (Leaf on 19.38). A few manuscripts add a line after 39, perhaps from a time when the Making of the Arms ended here (Leaf on 19.39).
- **19.43–44** — οἰήϊα "steering oars", only here in the Iliad. Leaf (on 19.43) would take the two lines for a gloss, were it not for the interest in feeding that runs through the book.
- **19.56–57** — ἦ ἄρ τι is a question, "was this then better for us both", with an ironical colour; others take it as a statement, "this would have been better", which Leaf (on 19.56) thinks weaker and against the particles. The printed ὅ τε is ὅτι, explaining τόδε, "that we two … raged"; Leaf allows the temporal ὅτε as well.
- **19.62** — ἐμεῦ ἀπομηνίσαντος, as 9.426, "while I nursed my wrath apart"; Nicias wrote ἄπο μην-, "apart from me in my anger", which the aorist does not suit (Leaf on 19.62).
- **19.68** — ἀσκελέως, only here: "unrelentingly or the like" to Leaf (on 19.68), who refers it to σκέλλω, "dried up", rigid like dried wood; ἀσκελὲς αἰεί is also used of anger at Od. 1.68. Rendered "stubbornly", to keep it apart from ἀσπερχές "relentlessly" (4.32, 16.61).
- **19.70** — ἔτι "once more", as in the old days (Leaf on 19.70); the sense of the καί that goes with it is not clear.
- **19.75** — μῆνιν ἀπειπόντος neglects the digamma that 19.35 (ἀποειπών) keeps; Heyne emended, Bentley rejected the line as superfluous (Leaf on 19.75).
- **19.76–84** — Agamemnon speaks "from where he sat, and not standing up in their midst" (77). Zenodotus did not have 77 and wrote 76 as "and lord Agamemnon stood up and spoke among them" (τοῖσι δʼ ἀνιστάμενος μετέφη κρείων Ἀγαμέμνων); the Massaliot and Chian texts had that line and another, "groaning over the wrath and suffering pains from his wound"; Didymus shows that Aristophanes had 77 (Leaf on 19.76). The question is tied to 79–80: without 77, "it is good to listen to the man who is on his feet, and not fitting to interrupt him"; with it, the scholia's view, which they ascribe to Aristarchus, that Agamemnon, who will not stand, speaks seated rather than through another man's mouth. Leaf rejects the second as perverse (and Agamemnon's wound is in the arm), takes ὑββάλλειν as "throw in a word", hence "interrupt", and thinks 77 an early and mistaken explanation of 79–80. The English keeps 77 and gives 79–80 the plain sense: "it is good to listen to a man when he stands up to speak, and it is not fitting / to interrupt". At 80 the vulgate has the accusatives ἐπιστάμενόν περ ἐόντα (Leaf on 19.80).
- **19.82** — βλάβεται "is hindered", also 19.166 and Od. 13.34 (Leaf on 19.82); λιγύς περ ἐὼν ἀγορητής, "clear-voiced orator though he is", as of Thersites (2.246); of Nestor, "the clear-voiced orator of the Pylians" (1.248).
- **19.87** — ἠεροφοῖτις: an ancient variant εἰαροπῶτις was explained "blood-drinking", from a Cyprian word εἶαρ "blood" (Leaf on 19.87); the Erinys brings ἄτη also at Od. 15.233–234.
- **19.88** — ἄτην cannot be scanned ἀϝάτην here, as it usually can; the variant ἀρήν, "curse", may be a conjecture (Leaf on 19.88).
- **19.90** — θεός, "a god", divine power, is not Ate herself; ancient variants were θεούς (with τέτυκται) and Hellanicus' θεόσδια, "god-given" (Leaf on 19.90).
- **19.91–94** — Ate in person, "Blind Folly" with a capital, as beside the Prayers (9.502–512), with which Leaf (on 19.91) counts the passage among the latest in the poems; Plato quotes 92–93 as Homer's (*Symposium* 195d). ἀᾶται "blinds" (also 129) is the only form of the verb that cannot be referred to ἀϝάζω, and the only transitive use of the middle, unless ἄσατο is transitive at 95 (Leaf on 19.91, 19.95). The scholia report 94 athetized as superfluous and badly put together (ἕτερον where ἄλλον was wanted); Leaf (on 19.94) keeps it and takes ἕτερον of "either party" to a quarrel.
- **19.95–133** — The story of Heracles' birth looks to Leaf (on 19.95) like an episode worked in from an independent Heracleia. At 95 the manuscripts have Ζῆνʼ, "she blinded Zeus", Aristarchus Ζεύς, "Zeus was blinded"; the printed text has Ζεύς, and the English follows it.
- **19.107** — ψευστήσεις, "you will prove false" (the manuscripts and Aristarchus); others read ψεύστης εἷς (Leaf on 19.107).
- **19.110–111** — πέσῃ μετὰ ποσσὶ γυναικός, "falls between a woman's feet", a naive phrase for being born (Leaf on 19.110). Leaf (on 19.111) finds Hera's guile not in a change of words but in Zeus' rash, unconditional oath, limited to one day.
- **19.113** — ἔπειτα, "in this" rather than "thereafter" (Leaf on 19.113).
- **19.114** — A scholium objects that Hera should have left "the starry sky": Agamemnon, Leaf (on 19.114) explains, knows too much for a mortal of the ways of the gods.
- **19.115** — Ἀχαιϊκόν: the digitized text printed Ἀχαιικόν without the diaeresis, the only such place in the Iliad (Ἀχαιϊκόν 9.141, 9.283, 9.521, 13.349, 15.218); corrected here.
- **19.115–123** — Ἄργος Ἀχαιϊκόν is the Argolid, as 9.141 (Leaf on 19.115). Sthenelus, son of Perseus, is not Diomedes' companion, the son of Capaneus; later writers give his wife several names (Leaf on 19.116). πρὸ φόως δέ, "out into the light", as 16.188; Aristarchus took πρό as "before his time" (Leaf on 19.118). Alcmene was Perseus' granddaughter through Electryon, so that Heracles and Eurystheus were both of the blood of Zeus (Leaf on 19.105).
- **19.119** — For Hera holding back the Eileithyiai Leaf (on 19.119) compares the *Homeric Hymn to Apollo* 99.
- **19.131** — ἔργʼ ἀνθρώπων, "the works of men", as 16.392; Leaf (on 19.131) finds the phrase strange here, the tilled fields standing for the world of men.
- **19.136–141** — 136–140 are obelized in the manuscript U (A is defective here). Nicole referred the obeli to 137–141, and Leaf (on 19.136) finds much in favour of rejecting those lines: 141 χθιζός, "yesterday", though the embassy was two nights before (Leaf on 19.141), the double mention of the gifts (140, 143), 137–138 copied from 9.119–120, and 139 against 142.
- **19.147–148** — How the infinitives are construed (on ἐθέλῃσθα, on πάρα σοι, or as imperatives) is uncertain; all come to the same sense (Leaf on 19.147).
- **19.149** — κλοτοπεύειν occurs nowhere else in Greek; the context points to "chatter" (Leaf on 19.149). Rendered "waste time in talk". ἄρεκτον "undone" (150) recurs only in a fragment of Simonides (Leaf on 19.150).
- **19.151–153** — Nicanor took ὡς (151) and ὧδε (153) as correlatives, "as a man will see Achilles … so let each of you"; with the full stop printed after 152, ὥς κε is final, "so that once again a man may see", as the English has it. Peppmüller moved 149–150 after 153; Heyne rejected 153 (Leaf on 19.151).
- **19.163** — ἄκμηνος occurs four times in Homer, all in Book 19 (163, 207, 320, 346); a scholium calls it Aeolic, for which there is no other evidence (Leaf on 19.163). The English has "fasting" for νῆστις, "unfed" for ἄκμηνος, and "going without" where it takes a genitive.
- **19.176–177** — = 9.275–276; 177 is omitted by many manuscripts, and τῆς refers to no one named before, as it does in Book 9 (Leaf on 19.176).
- **19.182–183** — ὅτε τις πρότερος χαλεπήνῃ: at 24.369 τις is the man who gave offense, but here the king himself gave it, so Leaf (on 19.182) understands that a king need not be ashamed to make amends when he was the first to quarrel. Ameis and Hentze join βασιλῆα ἄνδρα, "to make amends to a man of royal rank". The English, "when he was the first to grow angry", keeps the ambiguity of the Greek.
- **19.189** — For περ there is a well-supported variant γε, probably Aristarchus' reading (Leaf on 19.189).
- **19.193, 19.248** — κούρητες = κοῦροι, "young men", not the people the Curetes (9.529; Leaf on 19.193). Strabo quotes both places to show that Homer called young soldiers so (*Geography* 10.3.8).
- **19.194** — δῶρα ἐμῆς looks like a false archaism; Strabo (10.3.8) has δῶρα θοῆς, "from the swift ship", and ἐνεγκεῖν (Leaf on 19.194).
- **19.197** — κάπρον: competitors at Olympia swore by Zeus Horkios on the slices of a boar, and Pausanias, who describes the custom, quotes 19.266–268 for it (5.24.9–11; Leaf on 19.197). The Sun is a party to the oath as at 3.277.
- **19.205** — ὀτρύνετον is dual, "you two", Odysseus and Agamemnon (Leaf on 19.205).
- **19.208** — τεύξεσθαι, "make ready": Leaf (on 19.208) would read τεύξασθαι with the optative τεισαίμεθα.
- **19.212** — ἀνὰ πρόθυρον τετραμμένος, "turned toward the doorway": the dead man's feet are turned to the door, as for a departure; Leaf (on 19.212) compares Persius 3.105 and Pliny, *Natural History* 7.46.
- **19.216** — Πηλῆος: all the manuscripts have Πηλέος or Πηλέως; compare 1.489, 16.21 (Leaf on 19.216).
- **19.221–224** — Battle is a harvest of much straw and little grain: men soon have their fill of it and need food to bear such thankless work (Leaf on 19.221, after Valeton and Lendrum); ἄμητος "harvest", ὀλίγιστος an ironical understatement (Monro, quoted by Leaf on 19.223); 224 = 4.84.
- **19.225–229** — γαστέρι, "with the belly", ridicules fasting as a way to mourn in wartime (Leaf on 19.225). Cicero translates 226–229 (*Tusculan Disputations* 3.65). πόνοιο (227) is the toil of fasting to Leaf (on 19.226), the toil of battle to Monro.
- **19.229** — ἐπʼ ἤματι, "a single day", within a day's space, as 10.48 (Leaf on 19.229).
- **19.230–237** — Leaf (on 19.230) thinks the end of the speech out of place in Odysseus' mouth. At 235 he would put a colon after ὀτρυντύς, "this is the summons"; the printed text makes it "this summons will mean evil" (Leaf on 19.235).
- **19.247** — = 24.232; στήσας "weighing" (Leaf on 19.247).
- **19.252–256** — 252–253 = 3.271–272; ἀπὸ τρίχας ἀρξάμενος, "cutting the first hairs", as 3.273 (Leaf on 19.254). ἐπʼ αὐτόφιν (255), "in their places", is strange; Bekker conjectured αὐτόθι (Leaf on 19.255).
- **19.258–260** — Some read οἵ θʼ for αἵ θʼ, making Hades and Persephone, not the Erinyes, the punishers below the earth (Leaf on 19.258); Γῆ for γαῖα is late (Leaf on 19.259).
- **19.261** — ἐπένεικα, the indicative with μή in an oath, as 10.330; most manuscripts have the infinitive ἐπενεῖκαι, which cannot stand with ἐγώ (Leaf on 19.261).
- **19.262** — εὐνῆς πρόφασιν, "for the sake of my bed": πρόφασις is not necessarily a false pretext (Leaf on 19.262).
- **19.266–267** — Pausanias, quoting 19.266–268, reads ἀπὸ σφάραγον, "the throat", for ἀπὸ στόμαχον (5.24.11). The oath victim is neither burnt nor eaten but cast away, as 3.310; λαῖτμα, "gulf", is elsewhere Odyssean (Leaf on 19.267).
- **19.276** — αἰψηρήν is predicative, "quick to disperse at his word", hence "quickly dissolved the assembly"; λαιψηρήν is a variant here and at Od. 2.257 (Leaf on 19.276).
- **19.282–300** — Briseis' lament has non-epic expressions (Leaf on 19.282), among them the hiatus σε ἔλειπον (288). He says that tearing the skin (285) is not found elsewhere as a sign of grief; but a widow's cheeks are "torn in mourning", ἀμφιδρυφής (2.700, 11.393).
- **19.295–300** — The obeli in T and U suggest that Aristarchus athetized the lines: a marriage of Achilles to a captive is foreign to the poem; κουριδίη ἄλοχος is elsewhere a wife married as a maiden (Leaf on 19.298). The printed ἄξειν τʼ keeps Patroclus the subject; with δʼ, which Leaf prefers, Achilles would take her to Phthia.
- **19.302** — Πάτροκλον πρόφασιν, "for Patroclus outwardly": the usual sense; Heyne and Leaf (on 19.302) take πρόφασις for the occasion of a grief really felt, which wakes their own deeper sorrows, as 19.338–339 and 24.167.
- **19.306–308** — πρὶν … ὅ τι: πρίν looks ahead to "until the sun goes down" (308), as though πρὶν ἠέλιον δῦναι were to follow; a stop after 307 obscures the connection (Leaf on 19.306), hence the colon in the English.
- **19.314** — ἀνενείκατο, "he heaved a heavy sigh": he drew breath (Leaf on 19.314, comparing Herodotus 1.86 and 1.116).
- **19.326–337** — Aristarchus and Aristophanes athetized 327: Achilles could not doubt that his son lived, Scyros being near Troy, and θεοειδής did not suit; Didymus adds a variant that named the son Πυρῆς. Leaf (on 19.327) suspects the whole passage, 326–333 or 337, the Iliad knowing no son of Achilles except at the doubtful 24.467; he sets 332 beside Od. 11.506–509, and finds 334–335, where Peleus may be dead, ill matched with 322–323.
- **19.339** — ἔλειπον: the manuscripts have the singular ἔλειπε; editors print the plural with practically no support (Leaf on 19.339). "each remembering what he had left" serves either.
- **19.342** — ἀνδρὸς ἑῆος, "your brave man", takes ἑῆος in Aristarchus' sense, "good, brave" (at 1.393 and 15.138 the English has "your own son"); Zenodotus read ἑοῖο, "your own" (Leaf on 19.342).
- **19.343** — μέμβλετʼ: Leaf (on 19.343) takes it as μέμβλετο, a reduplicated aorist, "was Achilles no longer in your mind?"; the English has the present, μέμβλεται.
- **19.350** — ἅρπη, a bird of prey that cannot be identified; Aristotle seems to apply the name to a sea-bird (Leaf on 19.350; *History of Animals* 9).
- **19.354** — ἵκοιτο is Wolf's conjecture for the manuscripts' ἵκηται (Leaf on 19.354).
- **19.357** — Διός, "from Zeus", keeps the old sense "sky" (Leaf on 19.357, comparing 13.837); 358 = 15.171.
- **19.361** — κραταιγύαλοι, "with strong plates", only here; the line's awkward position marks it to Leaf (on 19.361) as a late addition.
- **19.362** — γέλασσε, "laughed": laughing and shining pass into one another (Leaf on 19.362).
- **19.365–368** — Aristarchus athetized the four lines, Achilles' gnashing of his teeth being grotesque; Dionysius Sidonius said he later removed the obeli, Ammonius that his second edition said nothing of it (Didymus, as Leaf reports on 19.365). Leaf keeps them, suspecting only that 368 was altered after the Making of the Arms was added.
- **19.369–373** — = 3.330–332, 334–335 (Leaf on 19.369). 374, the shield shining like the moon, has been suspected since Hermann and Heyne: the simile of 375–380 follows it, and the moon suggests a round shield (Leaf on 19.374).
- **19.375** — ἐκ πόντοιο: the light is seen by sailors on the sea (Leaf on 19.375).
- **19.382–383** — 382 is repeated from 22.315, where Achilles is running and περισσείοντο has its force, with τρυφάλεια awkwardly repeated; 383, in both places, alludes to the Making of the Arms (Leaf on 19.382). λόφον is "the crest" here, as at 18.612.
- **19.384** — δʼ ἕο αὐτοῦ, the emphatic reflexive; substantially Zenodotus' reading δʼ ἑοῦ (Leaf on 19.384).
- **19.385** — ἐφαρμόσσειε: the aorist suggests "whether he had fitted the arms on well"; the English takes it intransitively, with the dative, "whether it fitted him" (Leaf on 19.385).
- **19.386** — εὖτε, "like": Aristarchus is said to have read αὖτε, which would make them real wings (Leaf on 19.386).
- **19.387** — σύριγγος, "case", a socket to hold the spear, in this sense only here (Leaf on 19.387); 388–391 = 16.141–144.
- **19.392** — Ἄλκιμος, the familiar short form of Ἀλκιμέδων (Leaf on 19.392; 16.197, 17.467), again 24.474, 24.574.
- **19.401** — ἡνιοχῆα, "your charioteer": Achilles means himself, the fighter in the chariot, as Patroclus had been (403); Leaf (on 19.401) finds the singular more forcible, though the plural would be possible.
- **19.402** — ἕωμεν "have had our fill", from the root of ἄμεναι "to sate oneself" (21.70), intransitive (Leaf on 19.402).
- **19.404** — πόδας αἰόλος, "with flashing feet", only here: αἰόλος is used of worms, wasps and the gadfly, and joins rapid movement and glancing light; Leaf (on 19.404) compares the "flashing of feet" of the Phaeacian dancers (μαρμαρυγαὶ ποδῶν, Od. 8.265). See the note on 12.167.
- **19.407** — Aristarchus athetized the line as superfluous and as contradicting 418, since the god who gave the voice should have taken it away; Leaf (on 19.407) answers that the objection tells equally against 418. αὐδήεις, "with human voice", as of Ino, once a mortal (Od. 5.334), and of the goddess Circe (Od. 10.136).
- **19.411** — νωχελίῃ, "sloth", recurs in Greek only in Iamblichus; Euripides and later poets have νωχελής, "sluggish" (Leaf on 19.411).
- **19.416–417** — Aristarchus athetized the two lines as needless, objecting that φασί is used of what is not certainly known and does not suit a horse; Leaf (on 19.416) finds the reasons unconvincing (φασί often of things well known, as 19.96), but notes that the poet here seems not to know that the West Wind is Xanthus' own father (16.149–151).
- **19.418** — The Erinyes stop the horse's voice: elsewhere their functions are moral; here, Leaf (on 19.418) suggests, they are almost the law that orders the world, physical and moral, an idea he finds in Heraclitus (in Plutarch, *On Exile* 11, 604a: the sun will not overstep his measures, or the Erinyes, the helpers of Justice, will find him out) but thinks too refined for early epic; he would sooner lose 418 than 407. He compares Virgil, *Aeneid* 3.379–380.
- **19.421** — τό, Brandreth's conjecture for the manuscripts' τοι, is printed (Leaf on 19.421).
- **19.423** — ἅδην ἐλάσαι πολέμοιο, "given the Trojans their fill of war", as 13.315.
- **19.424** — Bentley conjectured ἐν πρώτοισιν ἐών, comparing 4.341 and 12.315 (Leaf on 19.424).
- **20.2** — ἀμφὶ σέ, Πηλέος υἱέ: the poet addresses Achilles, as he addresses Patroclus and Menelaus elsewhere and Apollo at 20.152. μάχης ἀκόρητον agrees with σέ, "you, insatiable of battle"; the variant ἀκόρητοι, of the Achaeans, has the commoner rhythm but less support, and Leaf (on 20.2) prefers it, since it is Achilles' men, not he, who might be thought to have had their fill of battle.
- **20.3** — The line is 11.56, where it has no verb either; here the verb is θωρήσσοντο of line 1, and the English supplies none ("were drawn up" at 11.56). Two manuscripts add a line to supply one (Leaf on 20.3).
- **20.5** — κρατὸς ἀπʼ Οὐλύμποιο: κράς, "head", of a mountaintop only here (Leaf on 20.5), rendered "summit".
- **20.7–9** — Oceanus alone stays away. Heyne (as Leaf on 20.7 reports) found the rivers and nymphs at a council of the gods stranger than Oceanus' absence, and thought 7–9 might have been added to account for the river Scamander among the gods in the battle. 20.9 recurs at Od. 6.124, after another verse on the nymphs (6.123).
- **20.18** — τῶν γὰρ νῦν ἄγχιστα μάχη πόλεμός τε δέδηε: literally "their battle and war have blazed up nearest". Leaf (on 20.18) takes the obvious sense, "has come to very close quarters", odd at the one pause in the fighting since Book 11, and explains it by the place of this prologue before the battle of the gods in Book 21; he rejects "is on the point of bursting out", "is kindled very near Olympus", "has come to a crisis" and "now most nearly concerns us". The English, "have blazed up at very close quarters", follows his obvious sense and keeps δαίω "blaze".
- **20.26** — μαχεῖται, a contracted future found nowhere else in Homer; Leaf (on 20.26) takes it as a sign of lateness rather than read μάχηται with van Leeuwen.
- **20.30** — ὑπέρμορον, as ὑπέρμορα (2.155), "against fate"; some divided ὑπὲρ μόρον, with the same sense (Leaf on 20.30). Zeus fears what fate does not allow: compare 2.155, 16.780, 17.321, 20.336 and the note on 6.487. Lines recorded in the T scholia were substituted for 29–30 by those who thought an omniscient god could not fear (Leaf on 20.30).
- **20.34** — ἐριούνης (also Od. 8.322), ἐριούνιος (20.72, four times in Book 24): usually derived from ἐρι- and ὀνίνημι, "the great helper"; Leaf (on 20.34) thinks a derivation from ἔριον, "wool", equally possible for the shepherds' god. Rendered "the great helper", apart from ἀκάκητα "the helper" (16.185), an epithet of Hermes as uncertain.
- **20.35** — ἐπὶ … κέκασται, tmesis, "excels"; not ἐπὶ φρεσί (Leaf on 20.35).
- **20.38–39** — κορυθαίολος is said only here of anyone but Hector (Leaf on 20.38); ἀκερσεκόμης, "of the unshorn hair", is not found again before the Hymn to Apollo and Pindar (Leaf on 20.39).
- **20.48** — The apodosis begins with ὦρτο δʼ Ἔρις; Aristarchus began it at αὖε δʼ Ἀθήνη, taking δέ there as redundant (Leaf on 20.48).
- **20.53** — θέων, "running", printed here; Aristarchus read θεῶν, "on Callicolone of the gods" (Leaf on 20.53, who finds it hardly credible). Callicolone, "Beauty Hill", is named only here and at 20.151; tradition made it the scene of the judgment of Paris, and Leaf thinks attempts to place it futile.
- **20.55** — ἐν δʼ αὐτοῖς, "among them": among men, Leaf thinks (on 20.55), though "among themselves", the gods, would suit 66; ῥήγνυντο ἔριδα, "made strife break out", is unique.
- **20.61–65** — εὐρώεντα "dank", from εὐρώς "mould", as of the house of Hades in the Odyssey (10.512, 23.322); some ancient readers took it as "spacious", from εὐρύς (Leaf on 20.64). On the Sublime 9.6 quotes 61–65 for the terror of the battle of the gods.
- **20.67–74** — ἔναντα and ἰά (for ἰούς) occur only here in Homer (Leaf on 20.67). Leaf sees no principle in the pairing of the gods except Hephaestus and the river, fire against water; he reports from the scholia an allegory of the pairs that Porphyry ascribed to Theagenes of Rhegium.
- **20.72** — σῶκος, only here (as a name, Σῶκος, 11.427): "strong" is a guess from the tragedians' σωκεῖν; Ἑρμῆς, the contracted name, is found four times in the Odyssey and nowhere else in the Iliad (Leaf on 20.72). Rendered "strong Hermes, the great helper".
- **20.77** — ἑ, Aristarchus' reading; most manuscripts have γε (Leaf on 20.77).
- **20.101–102** — ἶσον τείνειεν πολέμου τέλος, "were to stretch the outcome of war evenly": the image of the rope pulled from both ends (7.102, 13.358–360), with τέλος, "issue", for πεῖραρ. παγχάλκεος, "all of bronze", that is, invulnerable (Leaf on 20.101).
- **20.109** — ἀρειῇ, as 17.431, "threats"; Leaf (on 20.109) takes it here as "abuse", and on 17.431 allows "reviling" in all its places.
- **20.114** — θεούς, "the gods", here only Poseidon and Athena, as θεοὶ ἄλλοι at 149 only the Achaean party (Leaf on 20.114, 20.149).
- **20.125–128** — Athetized by Aristarchus as contradicting Zeus at 26, where the danger is that Achilles will be too strong, not that he will suffer; Leaf (on 20.125) answers that πάντες means only the gods of the Achaean side. For the thread spun at birth compare 24.209–210 and Od. 7.197–198 (Leaf on 20.128).
- **20.131** — χαλεποὶ δὲ θεοὶ φαίνεσθαι ἐναργεῖς, "gods are hard to face when they appear in plain sight": χαλεπός "hard to endure", the infinitive added loosely (Leaf on 20.131); compare Od. 16.161.
- **20.133** — παρʼ ἐκ νόον: the digitized text prints παρʼ ἐκ in two words, as nowhere else in Homer; the same phrase is printed παρὲκ νόον at 10.391, and Leaf's lemma (on 20.133) has παρὲκ. Left as printed; the English ("beyond good sense") and the lexicon read παρέξ.
- **20.138** — ἄρχωσι, a plural verb after two subjects joined by ἤ; Zenodotus read ἄρχῃσι (Leaf on 20.138).
- **20.143** — ἀναγκαίηφι, "under compulsion", has little manuscript support; the vulgate had ἀνάγκῃ ἶφι, and Leaf (on 20.143) asks whether ἀναλκείῃσι, "by their own feebleness", is better than both.
- **20.145–148** — The wall of Heracles: the story of Laomedon's daughter exposed to the sea monster is told, Leaf reports (on 20.145), by the A scholia and Apollodorus (2.5.9) after Hellanicus. ἀμφίχυτον "heaped-up", of earth.
- **20.155** — Ζεὺς δʼ ἥμενος ὕψι κέλευε, "though Zeus, sitting on high, bade them": the usual sense; Leaf (on 20.155), with Hentze, takes κέλευε absolutely, "was supreme over them", since Zeus had not told the gods to fight one another.
- **20.157** — κάρκαιρε, "rumbled", an imitative word found only here in Greek (Leaf on 20.156); "rang" is kept for κοναβίζω.
- **20.166** — ἀτίζων, "heedless", only here in Homer (Leaf on 20.166).
- **20.180–186** — Athetized by Aristarchus as mean in style and thought and unworthy of Achilles (Leaf on 20.180). The genitive τιμῆς τῆς Πριάμου, "the honor of Priam", is a late construction.
- **20.183** — ἀεσίφρων, "light of mind": referred since Buttmann to ἀάω, "blind"; Leaf (on 20.183) thinks the old derivation from ἄημι, "blow", "volatile", may be right.
- **20.195–198** — Athetized by Aristarchus: the last three lines are borrowed from 17.30–32, where Menelaus wants only the body of Patroclus and can let Euphorbus go, but Achilles, come for vengeance, should not (Leaf on 20.195).
- **20.202** — κερτομίας ἠδʼ αἴσυλα, "taunts and evil words" (202 = 433): αἴσυλος is always "unjust", "evil" (5.403; Leaf on 20.202), and "unseemly" is kept for ἀεκήλιος and ἀεικής. Leaf thinks ἠμὲν … ἠδέ wants an antithesis and would read αἴσιμα with Düntzer, "abuse and seemly speech" alike.
- **20.204** — πρόκλυτα, "the tales mortal men have told of old": "heard in old times", or "handed on" by tradition (Leaf on 20.204); kept apart from τηλεκλυτός "far-famed".
- **20.205–209** — Athetized by Aristarchus as needless, since both men know each other's lineage (Leaf on 20.205). 208–209 repeat 5.247–248 with the first person, and are rendered as there.
- **20.207** — ἁλοσύδνης, "daughter of the sea": recurs only at Od. 4.404, where the seals are the brood of "the fair ἁλοσύδνη"; Leaf (on 20.207) takes -ύδνη as a stem of ὕδωρ, "daughter of the salt water".
- **20.215** — Δάρδανον αὖ: Leaf (on 20.215) printed ἄρ, holding that αὖ cannot begin a narrative; the English renders neither.
- **20.218** — ὑπωρείας, "slopes", the low hill country below Ida; Plato quotes 216–218 in the Laws (681e).
- **20.219** — Erichthonius, an Athenian hero in a Trojan genealogy: Leaf (on 20.219) reports Fick's view that the passage was made at Athens when the Athenians were gaining a footing at Sigeum, and Strabo (13.1.48) reports that later writers took an Erichthonius among the founders of both peoples as a sign of their kinship.
- **20.224** — ἵππῳ … κυανοχαίτῃ: κυανοχαίτης is elsewhere Poseidon's epithet, "dark-haired" (13.563, 15.174; 20.144); of the stallion whose likeness the North Wind takes it is rendered "dark-maned". Leaf (on 20.224) sees no special point in it for a horse.
- **20.227** — ἀνθερίκων: "ears" of grain, as the A scholia explain it; later Greek uses the word of the asphodel stalk, and Leaf (on 20.227) sees no reason why it should not be so here. A Hesiodic fragment says the same of Iphiclus.
- **20.229** — ἄκρον ἐπὶ ῥηγμῖνος, "on the very crests of the breakers"; Ahrens read ἄκρον ἐπὶ ῥηγμῖνα (Leaf on 20.229).
- **20.234** — ἀνηρείψαντο, "snatched up": Döderlein read ἀνηρέψαντο, from the root of ἁρπάζω (Leaf on 20.234). Aristotle cites Ganymede "to pour wine for Zeus" (Διὶ οἰνοχοεύειν), though the gods drink no wine, as a word used by custom of the language (Poetics 25, 1461a29–31).
- **20.235** — = Od. 15.251; Aristarchus rejected it there and kept it here (Leaf on 20.235).
- **20.245** — ὑσμίνῃ δηϊοτῆτος, "the battle of combat": ὑσμίνη with a genitive is unique in Homer (Heyne, as Leaf on 20.245 reports).
- **20.247** — ἑκατόζυγος, "of a hundred benches", only here; ἄροιτο, "carry", the only place where ἄρνυμαι is not "win" (Leaf on 20.247).
- **20.249** — ἐπέων … νομός, "the pasture of words": Leaf (on 20.249) takes νομός as the field where words find their food; the scholia explained "distribution" (νέμησις).
- **20.250** — Compare Hesiod, Works and Days 721, "if you speak ill, you may soon hear worse yourself".
- **20.251–255** — Athetized by Aristarchus as untimely after 244 and unworthy of the speakers; Leaf (on 20.251) finds the comparison with the women the liveliest passage in the speech, though νεικεῦσι is a late form.
- **20.255** — πόλλʼ ἐτεά τε καὶ οὐκί, "many things, true and not true"; Aristarchus read πολλά τʼ ἐόντα καὶ οὐκί (Leaf on 20.255).
- **20.259–260** — δεινῷ σάκει … σμερδαλέῳ, "the dread shield, the terrible shield": a reading δινῷ is reported, and Aristarchus read σάκεϊ ἔλασʼ (Leaf on 20.259); Leaf thinks σμερδαλέῳ weak and would consider the adverb σμερδαλέον (on 20.260).
- **20.269–272** — Athetized by Aristarchus: the five layers of bronze, tin and gold contradict 18.481, where the layers are of the shield itself, and the gold in the middle could not stop a spear that had pierced two layers (Leaf on 20.269, who calls the lines spurious).
- **20.272** — Aristotle quotes the half-line as τῇ ῥʼ ἔσχετο χάλκεον ἔγχος, "bronze" where the Iliad has μείλινον "ash", for a word that seems to signify a contradiction: in how many senses could the spear be "stopped" there (Poetics 25, 1461a31–35)?
- **20.282** — ἄχος … ὀφθαλμοῖσι, "grief was poured over his eyes", an unusual phrase; Bentley proposed ἀχλύς, "mist", as at 20.321 (Leaf on 20.282).
- **20.289** — τό οἱ ἤρκεσε λυγρὸν ὄλεθρον, "on the shield that had kept grim destruction from him": an aorist indicative without κε, and τό agrees with σάκος, the shield that had saved Achilles at 268 (Leaf on 20.289), not "which would have kept".
- **20.302** — μόριμον, "fated", only here in Homer; most manuscripts have the unmetrical μόρσιμον (Leaf on 20.302).
- **20.306–308** — Poseidon's prophecy that Aeneas and his children's children shall rule the Trojans. ἔχθηρε is printed without the augment; Strabo (13.1.53) quotes 306–308 with ἤχθηρε, the form of the Odyssey (11.437, 11.560, 19.364), and reports that "some write Αἰνείαο γένος πάντεσσιν ἀνάξει … meaning the Romans"; no manuscript has it (Leaf on 20.307). The Hymn to Aphrodite says the same (196–197).
- **20.312** — Leaf (on 20.312) thinks the line interpolated, to give ἐάσῃς a construction it does not need.
- **20.315–317** — = 21.374–376 (with καιομένη, καίωσι); some editors reject 316–317 here (Leaf on 20.315).
- **20.322–324** — Athetized by Aristarchus: Achilles' spear went through the shield and stood in the ground (276–280), so Poseidon could not pull it out of the shield (Leaf on 20.322).
- **20.329** — The Caucones are not in the Trojan Catalogue (see the note on 10.428–431); the Odyssey has Caucones too, to whom Athena goes from Pylos (Od. 3.366), and Herodotus (1.147.1) names Pylian Caucones, descended from Codrus, among the kings of the Ionians.
- **20.332** — ἀτέοντα, "recklessly", only here in Homer (Herodotus 7.223.4 uses ἀτέοντες of the Spartans at Thermopylae); Leaf (on 20.332) would accept the variant χατέοντα, "lacking the gods".
- **20.370** — κολούει; Leaf (on 20.370) would read the future κολούσει.
- **20.381–395** — Leaf (on 20.381) suspects the Iphition episode of later origin: the short vowels of Ὀτρυντεΐδης, the borrowing from the Catalogue (2.865–866), and "most terrible of all men", said elsewhere of Achilles himself. Strabo (13.4.6) reports that some added "beneath snowy Tmolus, in the rich land of Hyde" after 2.866, and that no Hyde was to be found among the Lydians.
- **20.392** — Strabo (13.4.5) puts the Gygaean lake "named by the poet" forty stadia from Sardis, later called Coloe, with a temple of Artemis Coloene, and says that the Hyllus, now called the Phrygius, flows into the Hermus.
- **20.404** — Ἑλικώνιον ἄνακτα, "the Heliconian lord": Poseidon of Helice in Achaea, Leaf thinks (on 20.404), or the Panionian festival of Heliconian Poseidon at Mycale. Strabo (8.7.2) reports that some took the line for that sacrifice and inferred that the poet lived after the Ionian migration, and others referred it to Helice; Herodotus (1.148.1) names the Panionium as sacred to Poseidon Heliconius. The English keeps the adjective.
- **20.420** — ποτὶ γαίη: the digitized text prints γαίη without the iota subscript, a form found nowhere else in Homer (ποτὶ γαίῃ 4.112; προτὶ γαίῃ 22.64); as at 11.729, the English and the lexicon read the dative, and the Greek is left as printed.
- **20.421** — κάρ ῥά οἱ ὀφθαλμῶν κέχυτʼ ἀχλύς; a variant κέχυτο χλόος, "pallor", has good manuscripts, and Apollonius seems to have read it (Leaf on 20.421).
- **20.424** — ἀνεπᾶλτο is accented as from ἅλλομαι (ἆλτο), ἀνέπαλτο at 8.85 and 23.694 as from ἀναπάλλω; "leapt up" serves either.
- **20.437** — πάροιθεν, "at the point": local, "in front"; the T scholia and Eustathius took it as "before now", of Patroclus' death (Leaf on 20.437).
- **20.447** — Omitted by most manuscripts; Leaf (on 20.444) thinks it interpolated from the three parallel passages (5.438, 16.705, 16.786).
- **20.463–468** — Leaf (on 20.463) suspects 463–468 (γλυκύθυμος, the poet's own judgment on Achilles); 466 = Od. 3.146.
- **20.484** — Πείρεω is not a Homeric form; Brandreth's Πειρόου would make Rhigmus the son of the Thracian leader Peiroos of 2.844 and 4.520 (Leaf on 20.484).
- **20.486** — νηδύϊ, "in his belly"; many manuscripts have πνεύμονι, "in his lung", from 4.528 (Leaf on 20.486).
- **20.495–503** — Rejected by several editors, because 499–502 repeat 11.534–537 and Achilles, fighting on foot, is suddenly in his chariot; Leaf (on 20.495) keeps them.
- **20.497** — λέπτʼ ἐγένοντο, "it is husked", from λέπω, "peel" (Leaf on 20.497).
- **21.1–2** — = 14.433–434, 24.692–693. The ford of the Xanthus is named only in this formal couplet (Leaf on 21.4).
- **21.3** — διατμήξας, "split", of cutting the Trojans into two bodies; Bentley took it as "crossing", as in Od. 5.409 (Leaf on 21.3). πεδίον δέ is the suffix of direction printed apart (πεδίονδε), "across the plain"; so again πεδίον δέ "toward the plain" (563), ποταμὸν δέ (13, 120), χέρσον δέ "onto the dry land" (238), Οὔλυμπον δέ "to Olympus" (438).
- **21.4** — Ἀχαιοί: most manuscripts have οἱ ἄλλοι, from 6.41 and 21.554 (Leaf on 21.4).
- **21.6–7** — πεφυζότες "in panic", a perfect participle found only in this book (6, 528, 532) and at 22.1 (Leaf on 21.6). Hera's mist is not mentioned again; Leaf takes it to hold back the Trojans, and reports that Düntzer struck out ἠέρα … ἐρυκέμεν.
- **21.8** — βαθύρροος, "deep-streaming", is said elsewhere only of Oceanus (Leaf on 21.8).
- **21.12** — ῥιπῆς πυρός "the rush of fire", as of the North Wind (15.171); ἠερέθονται "flutter up into the air", Leaf's "take wing". The scholia call the driving of locusts with fire a Cypriot practice, and Strabo reports it among the locust-eaters of Aethiopia (Leaf on 21.12).
- **21.20–21** — = 10.483–484, with τύπτε for κτεῖνε and ὕδωρ for γαῖα (Leaf on 21.20).
- **21.22** — μεγακήτεος, "huge-bellied", of the dolphin; of ships "huge in the hull" (8.222). Leaf (on 21.22) rejects van Leeuwen's ἐλλοί for ἄλλοι: the poet counts the dolphin among the fishes.
- **21.28** — ποινήν "the penalty", the word's fixed rendering; Leaf (on 21.28) "blood-price", as 18.498. The twelve young men are promised at 18.336–337 and killed at 23.175–176.
- **21.31** — The straps (ἱμάντες) are no doubt the leather belts of the young men's tunics; they wore no corselet (Leaf on 21.31).
- **21.37–38** — ἐρινεὸν … ὄρπηκας, "from a wild fig tree … young shoots", the whole and the part (Leaf on 21.37, who reports Agar's ἐρινεοῦ); the young branches bend into a chariot's rails (ἄντυγες).
- **21.40** — ἐπέρασσε "sold" (περάω, "make to pass over"), in the Iliad only in this book (40, 58, 78, 102, 454) (Leaf on 21.40). 21.58 πεπερημένος, "sold".
- **21.41** — The son of Jason is Euneus, the son of Hypsipyle (7.468–469), and the price was the silver mixing bowl of 23.741–747 (Leaf on 21.41).
- **21.43** — This Eetion of Imbros appears only here; Ἴμβριος distinguishes him from Andromache's father (Leaf on 21.43). Arisbe is on the Hellespont (2.836).
- **21.45** — οἷσι φίλοισιν, "with his friends": comitative rather than locative, as 7.61 and Od. 13.61 (Leaf on 21.45).
- **21.48** — νέεσθαι, "on his way" (to the house of Hades), one of the few places where the verb has lost the sense of a happy return (Leaf on 21.48).
- **21.49–53** — The sentence begun at 49 is interrupted to explain how Achilles knew Lycaon was unarmed, and its apodosis comes at 53 (Leaf on 21.50).
- **21.59** — πόντος ἁλὸς πολιῆς, "the deep of the gray sea" (Leaf on 21.59).
- **21.62–63** — κεῖθεν, "from there", the world below, by a euphemism (Leaf on 21.62). γῆ φυσίζοος, "the life-giving earth", as φυσίζοος αἶα (3.243); Leaf (on 21.63) discusses Brandreth's γαῖα φυσίζωος and reports that the T scholia found the epithet unfitting for the earth that holds the dead.
- **21.67–70** — At 17 Achilles left his spear on the bank; here he has it (Leaf on 21.67). Though the verb is οὐτάμεναι, "stab", the spear flies over Lycaon's back into the ground, as at 20.279–280 (Leaf on 21.68).
- **21.73** — Didymus reports that the line was not in Aristarchus' editions; the manuscripts have it (Leaf on 21.73).
- **21.75** — ἀντί τοί εἰμʼ ἱκέταο, "I am as good as a suppliant to you": having eaten at Achilles' table, Lycaon claims the standing of one (Leaf on 21.75, with ἀντί as Od. 8.546).
- **21.80** — λύμην, aorist indicative, "I was ransomed", not an optative, as the ancient commentators took it (Leaf on 21.80). τρὶς τόσσα, "three times as much", the price Eetion paid.
- **21.86–87** — Ἄλταο … Ἄλτεω: the genitive repeated in two forms; ἀνάσσει, "rules", in the present (the variant ἄνασσε is perhaps a correction after 20.92). ἐπὶ Σατνιόεντι, "on the Satnioeis", for the vulgate ὑπό; Leaf (on 21.87) reports Strabo's remark that some write ὑπό, wrongly. αἰπήεσσαν, "steep", only here.
- **21.89** — δειροτομήσεις, "you will cut the throats of", of the helpless victim (Leaf on 21.89); again 555.
- **21.92** — ἔσσεται or ἔσσεαι ("you will be an evil to me"), both well attested (Leaf on 21.92).
- **21.94–96** — Bayfield condemned the three lines (Leaf on 21.94): 94 = 1.297, 96 = 17.204, and the plea that Lycaon is not of the same womb as Hector comes oddly after the death of his full brother Polydorus. ὁμογάστριος (95), "of the same womb"; Zenodotus read ἰογάστριος (Leaf on 21.95; compare 24.47).
- **21.99** — πιφαύσκεο, "offer", perhaps "tender" (Leaf on 21.99 and 18.500).
- **21.101** — τι, "somehow", gives a colour of reluctance to the admission (Leaf on 21.101).
- **21.106–107** — φίλος: the scholia saw mockery of Lycaon's claim to hospitality, Leaf (on 21.106) real pity. Leaf (on 21.107) calls 107 a famous line. Plutarch (*Alexander* 54.1), after Hermippus, tells that Callisthenes, seeing the king turned against him, said it to himself two or three times as he went away (Leaf has him use it to warn Alexander); Lucretius adapts it, bonus Ancus … qui melior multis quam tu fuit (3.1025–1026), and Leaf quotes his lines on the death of Epicurus (3.1042–1045).
- **21.111** — δείλη, "evening", only here in Homer; the scholia put a stop after ἔσσεται (Leaf on 21.111).
- **21.112** — Ἄρῃ for Ἄρηϊ, "in the work of Ares", as στυγερῷ … Ἄρηϊ "the hateful work of Ares" (2.385, 18.209).
- **21.122–123** — σʼ … ὠτειλὴν αἷμʼ, a double accusative; ἀκηδέες, "caring nothing for you", or, with Monro, "without mourning rites" (Leaf on 21.122–123).
- **21.126–127** — "Leaping along the wave, one will dart up under the dark ripple": ὑπαΐξει, "dart up to (or beneath)"; Leaf (on 21.126) suspects a corruption, and reports the variant ὑπαλύξει with Philetas' and Callistratus' explanation that a fish fattened on the fat escapes the cold.
- **21.130–135** — Aristophanes athetized the six lines, Didymus says, as added by those who did not see why the river is angry (146 says why); Aristarchus perhaps agreed (Leaf on 21.130). δηθά with the presents ἱερεύετε, καθίετε, "you have long sacrificed" (Leaf on 21.131). Live horses thrown into a river have no parallel in Homer (Leaf on 21.131).
- **21.137** — πόνοιο, "toil"; Leaf (on 21.137) thinks φόνοιο, "slaughter", rather more suitable.
- **21.141–143** — Πηλεγόνος: Leaf (on 21.141) takes Pelegon for a Thracian eponym; neither he nor Acessamenus recurs, but the Odyssey has another Periboea loved by a god (Od. 7.56–62). εὐρυρέεθρος, "of the broad streams", only here.
- **21.146** — δαϊκταμένων, "slain in battle", only here and 301 (Leaf on 21.146).
- **21.148–149** — = 6.121–122; Leaf (on 21.148) finds 148 out of place after 144.
- **21.150** — τίς πόθεν εἰς ἀνδρῶν, two questions in one, as Od. 1.170 (Leaf on 21.150).
- **21.154–158** — Asteropaeus is not among the Paeonian leaders of the Catalogue (2.848–850), where the Paeonians are archers; δολιχεγχέας, "with their long spears", is only here. Some texts added a line after 2.848 to name him (Leaf on 21.154); 158 is adapted from 2.850 (Leaf on 21.158).
- **21.162–163** — δούρασιν ἀμφίς, "with two spears at once"; περιδέξιος, "ambidextrous", only here (Leaf on 21.162–163).
- **21.165** — = 20.268, where the digitized text has a comma after ἐρύκακε; it is left as printed here, and the English is the same.
- **21.166–167** — πῆχυν … χειρός, "the forearm of his right arm": χείρ the forearm, as 11.252, 20.479; ἐπιγράβδην, "grazed", only here, as ἐπέγραψε (4.139) and ἐπιλίγδην (17.599). The arm was raised for the cast, so the spear, grazing it, passed over his body; ἣ δʼ, feminine, of the spear, as if the poet had αἰχμή in mind (Leaf on 21.166–167).
- **21.169** — ἰθυπτίωνα, "straight-flying", only here; Zenodotus read ἰθυκτίωνα (Leaf on 21.169).
- **21.172** — μεσσοπαγές, "fixed halfway"; Aristarchus read μεσσοπαλές (Leaf on 21.172).
- **21.176** — ἐρύσσασθαι, as printed; Leaf's text has the future ἐρύσσεσθαι (on 21.176, with his note on 7.36), as in the same line at Od. 21.125. The English, "eager to draw it out", serves either.
- **21.177** — μεθῆκε βίης, "held back from the effort", Bekker's reading from Od. 21.126; the manuscripts have βίῃ (Leaf on 21.177).
- **21.184–191** — παισίν, plural of a general truth (Leaf on 21.185). τῶ, "So", or, as a relative, "by so much" (Leaf on 21.190). ἁλιμυρήεις, "that murmur to the sea", only here and Od. 5.460 (Leaf on 21.190).
- **21.192** — πάρα: the river beside him is the Scamander, though the context suggests the Axius (Leaf on 21.192).
- **21.194–197** — Achelous is named in Homer only here; the river of 24.616 is another (Leaf on 21.194). Zenodotus omitted 195, leaving Achelous the source of all waters; Aristarchus, who kept it (in Homer Oceanus is the parent of rivers), read οὔτε (Leaf on 21.195).
- **21.203–204** — ἀμφεπένοντο, "busied themselves about him", properly of tending the wounded, here ironical; the eels are set apart from the fishes (Leaf on 21.203). δημὸν … ἐπινεφρίδιον, "the fat about his kidneys"; ἐπινεφρίδιος only here.
- **21.213** — ἐκ … φθέγξατο: Leaf (on 21.213) reads the compound ἐκφθέγξατο, "called out from".
- **21.215** — ἀνδρῶν with περί; Leaf (on 21.215) suspects the line.
- **21.223** — ἔσται ταῦτα, "So it shall be": what Achilles grants is not plain (Leaf on 21.223).
- **21.229–232** — The river's reproach to Apollo has been condemned, since Apollo is not there and nothing comes of it; Leaf (on 21.229) defends it as a reproach. δείελος ὀψὲ δύων, "the evening, late in its setting", perhaps the evening star or the setting sun (Leaf on 21.232).
- **21.237** — μεμυκὼς ἠΰτε ταῦρος, "bellowing like a bull": rivers were figured as bulls (Leaf on 21.237).
- **21.245** — γεφύρωσεν, "bridged", perhaps rather "dammed" (Leaf on 21.245, with 5.89).
- **21.249** — ἀκροκελαινιόων, only here: "darkening at the crest" (Leaf: "with black surface").
- **21.252** — μέλανος τοῦ θηρητῆρος, "the black hunter". Leaf (on 21.252) reports the variants μελανόστου ("black-boned", ascribed to Aristotle, more likely to Democritus), μελανόσσου ("black-eyed") and Ahrens' μελανόρσου ("black-tailed"), and Philetas' ὄμματα for οἴματα.
- **21.257–262** — The irrigation simile has no parallel elsewhere in Homer (Leaf on 21.257); προαλεῖ, "sloping", only here (Leaf on 21.262).
- **21.274** — τι πάθοιμι, "I could suffer anything", in the Attic sense of dying: let come what may, if only he is saved from the river (Leaf on 21.274).
- **21.278** — Achilles' death by Apollo's arrows: compare Hector's prophecy (22.359–360). Leaf (on 21.278) compares Sophocles, Philoctetes 334–336.
- **21.282–283** — ἔναυλος, "a torrent" in a gully; ἀποέρσῃ, "sweeps away", a word the scholia on 329 call Cyprian (Leaf on 21.283).
- **21.287** — τοῖσι, "among them", from the formula (7.445), though Poseidon speaks to Achilles alone (Leaf on 21.287).
- **21.290** — Athetized because a god in a man's shape should not name himself; Leaf (on 21.290) defends it: Achilles must know his help is divine.
- **21.295** — Ἰλιόφι used as a genitive after τείχεα, "a false archaism" (Leaf on 21.295).
- **21.301** — δαῒ κταμένων, "slain in battle", in two words, as A writes with Herodian, where 146 prints δαϊκταμένων (Leaf on 21.146); the digitized δαὶ, without the diaeresis, would be the particle, and is corrected.
- **21.302** — πλῶον, "was floating", of armor lifted by the flood (Leaf on 21.302).
- **21.317–323** — τὰ τεύχεα καλά, "that fine armor" of his (Leaf on 21.317). 319 εἰλύσω or ἰλύσω; χέραδος, "gravel", neuter (Leaf on 21.319). Friedländer thought 320–321 and 322–323 two variants combined (Leaf on 21.320). τυμβοχόης, Crates' reading, "of a heaped-up tomb"; Aristarchus read τυμβοχοῆσʼ, an infinitive (Leaf on 21.323).
- **21.331** — The A scholia record an athetesis for the epithet (Hera should not name her son's lameness when she asks his help); Leaf (on 21.331) calls the line indispensable and gives Cobet's explanation. κυλλοπόδιον, vocative of κυλλοποδίων (18.371, 20.270).
- **21.332** — ἠΐσκομεν, "we judged": the ironical imperfect, after the pairing of the gods at 20.73–74, fire against water (Leaf on 21.332).
- **21.335** — εἴσομαι, "I will go": Leaf (on 21.335) takes it from (ϝ)ίεμαι, "I will hasten"; Zenodotus took it as "I shall know", and read ἤ, "whether", and ὄρσασα in 336. Hera's winds are never sent.
- **21.344** — = 236; Leaf (on 21.344) thinks it interpolated.
- **21.347** — ἐθείρῃ, "tends": the word's meaning is unknown, and the context suggests it (Leaf on 21.347).
- **21.356** — ἲς ποταμοῖο, "the force of the river", rendered as ἲς ἀνέμοιο "the force of the wind" (15.383, 17.739). The periphrasis with ἴς, common in the Odyssey, does not recur in the Iliad, which uses βίη (Leaf on 21.356).
- **21.361** — φῆ after a speech for the usual ἦ, only here (Leaf on 21.361).
- **21.362–364** — The cauldron melts the fat of a hog: μελδόμενος, "as it melts"; Leaf (on 21.363) reports the variant μελδομένου (the hog being melted down). ἀμβολάδην, "bubbling up"; κάγκανα, "dry".
- **21.374–376** — = 20.315–317.
- **21.386** — ἄητο, "was blown", of the heart blown about by gusts of passion (Leaf on 21.386).
- **21.388** — σάλπιγξεν, "trumpeted", only here: the din echoed from the sky (Leaf on 21.388, comparing Theogony 679–680); the trumpet itself appears only in a simile (18.219).
- **21.389–390** — Zeus laughs outright only here and at 508 (Leaf on 21.390).
- **21.394** — κυνάμυια, "dog-fly", only here and 421: the dog for shamelessness, the fly for boldness (17.570), as Aristonicus explains (Leaf on 21.394, who prints κυνόμυια).
- **21.395** — θάρσος ἄητον, "your gale of daring": ἄητος is doubtful (compare αἴητον 18.410), perhaps "blown about", from ἄητο above; others "insatiable" (Leaf on 21.395).
- **21.397** — πανόψιον, "seen by all", against the goddess unseen at 5.856; Antimachus read ὑπονόσφιον, "stealthy", now found in a papyrus (Leaf on 21.397).
- **21.401** — The aegis that not even Zeus' thunderbolt overpowers; most manuscripts have ἀσπίδα, but θυσσανόεσσα is said only of the aegis (Leaf on 21.401).
- **21.407** — ἑπτὰ … πέλεθρα, "seven plethra" (a plethron is about a hundred feet); Tityos covers nine in the Odyssey (11.577) (Leaf on 21.407).
- **21.410** — νηπύτιε, "Little fool", as an address peculiar to this book (410, 441, 474, 585) (Leaf on 21.410); νήπιε "Fool" (21.99).
- **21.412** — μητρὸς ἐρινύας, "the Erinyes of your mother": here hardly more than her curses (Leaf on 21.412; Od. 11.280). Ares' desertion of Hera's side is denounced at 5.832.
- **21.416** — The Separatists argued that the poet of the Iliad made Aphrodite the companion of Ares and the poet of the Odyssey the wife of Hephaestus (the scholia, as Leaf on 21.416 reports).
- **21.434** — Leaf (on 21.434) thinks the line interpolated from 1.595.
- **21.441–457** — The service of Poseidon and Apollo to Laomedon is also told at 7.452–453, where both gods build the wall (Leaf on 21.442, 21.446). θητεύσαμεν, "served for hire" (Od. 18.357–358) (Leaf on 21.444). ἀπολεψέμεν, "lop off", Aristarchus' reading for the vulgate ἀποκοψέμεν (Leaf on 21.455); τηλεδαπάων (454) for the city editions' θηλυτεράων.
- **21.460** — The digitized text ends Poseidon's speech without a stop; one is supplied.
- **21.464–466** — The leaves recall 6.146; Leaf (on 21.464) calls the mixture of leaves "blazing with life" and "eating the fruit of the field" a medley.
- **21.471** — Athetized as superfluous (Aristonicus, as Leaf on 21.471 reports).
- **21.473** — μέλεον, "for nothing", an adverb, as 10.480, 16.336 (Leaf on 21.473).
- **21.475–477** — Athetized, since Apollo is no warrior (the scholia, as Leaf on 21.475 reports); Leaf keeps them.
- **21.480** — Unknown to Eustathius and adapted from 2.277: Aristonicus understood προσέφη from 478 (Leaf on 21.480).
- **21.483** — λέοντα γυναιξί, "a lion to women": Leaf (on 21.483) takes the lion as a figure of death and Artemis as a goddess of death for women (6.205, 6.428).
- **21.487–489** — The sentence breaks off and the deed supplies the rest (Nicanor, as Leaf on 21.487 reports); the English marks the break.
- **21.492** — ἐντροπαλιζομένην, "as she twisted this way and that": Artemis turns her head aside from the blows; the variant ἐντροπαλιζομένη, of Hera, is inferior (Leaf on 21.492).
- **21.502–503** — καμπύλα τόξα, "the curved bow", though τόξα includes the arrows; μετὰ στροφάλιγγι κονίης, "in the whirl of dust", from 16.775 (Leaf on 21.502).
- **21.509–510** — = 5.373–374; 510 is omitted by almost all manuscripts (Leaf on 21.509).
- **21.515** — The digitized text printed a quotation mark at the start of the line, as though the narrative were speech, and closed it after Priam's words at 536, which it gave single marks; the slip is corrected here, and Priam's words (531–536) have the double marks of speech.
- **21.522–525** — Adapted from 18.207–210 (Leaf on 21.522). θεῶν δέ ἑ μῆνις ἀνῆκε, "and the wrath of the gods has sent it up", may be a parenthesis; Bothe omitted 524 (Leaf on 21.523).
- **21.526** — θείου, "divine", of a tower built by a god (Leaf on 21.526).
- **21.531** — The gates are presumably the Scaean gate (Leaf on 21.531).
- **21.535** — ἐπανθέμεναι, Aristarchus' reading, "put back"; some city texts had ἐπʼ ἂψ θέμεναι (Leaf on 21.535).
- **21.538** — φάος, "a light", safety for the fugitives, as 6.6 (Leaf on 21.538).
- **21.541** — καρχαλέοι, "parched", only here before Apollonius (Leaf on 21.541).
- **21.548** — θανάτοιο βαρείας χεῖρας, "the heavy hands of Death", the reading of all the manuscripts: κῆρας, found as early as Eustathius, has no known manuscript support, and Leaf (on 21.548) accepts the bold personification.
- **21.549** — φηγῷ, "the oak", presumably the oak by the Scaean gate (5.693) (Leaf on 21.549).
- **21.552–570** — Agenor's deliberation follows the pattern of 11.404–410 and is closest to Hector's (22.99–130); 562 = 11.407 = 22.122 (Leaf on 21.552).
- **21.555** — ἀνάλκιδα, "a coward", with its contempt (Leaf on 21.555; 8.153, 9.35).
- **21.558** — πεδίον Ἰλήϊον, "the Ilean plain", only here; the B scholia explain it as the plain by the tomb of Ilus, Crates read Ἰδήϊον, and Leaf (on 21.558) thinks the line corrupt.
- **21.567** — πόλεος, as the digitized text prints it, scanned as two syllables; Leaf prints πόλιος, whose synizesis is very rare (only 2.811, in the same phrase), and reports Menrad's conjecture πρόσθεν πόλιος (Leaf on 21.567).
- **21.568–570** — Achilles' invulnerability is post-Homeric (Leaf on 21.568). 570 was athetized as added by someone who thought a verb was missing, and because it contradicts Agenor's hope (Leaf on 21.570).
- **21.575** — ὑλαγμόν, "the baying of the hounds"; some read κυνυλαγμόν (Leaf on 21.575).
- **21.587** — οἳ καί, Aristarchus' reading, for οἵ κε (Leaf on 21.587).
- **21.594** — Leaf (on 21.594) thinks the line added to recall the god's making of the arms; A omits it.
- **21.611** — σαώσαι, Aristarchus' reading, for σάωσαν (Leaf on 21.611).
- **22.1** — πεφυζότες "in panic", as 21.6, 21.528, 21.532 (Leaf on 22.1; see the note on 21.6–7).
- **22.2** — ἀπεψύχοντο, "let the sweat dry from them": the scholia report both ἀπ- and ἀνεψύχοντο, and Aristarchus is said to have written ἀνεψύχοντο; Leaf (on 22.2) holds that ἀπό is the preposition required, as the verb recurs without a variant at 11.621 and 21.561. ἀκέοντο, of thirst, "quenched", a unique use: the verb is regularly used of healing wounds, and also of mending ships and repairing an error (Leaf on 22.2).
- **22.4** — σάκεʼ ὤμοισι κλίναντες, "leaning their shields against their shoulders", the formation of 11.593 and 13.488, there made to receive a charge; how it serves an advance is unclear, and Leaf (on 22.4) suggests a reconnaissance made with every precaution.
- **22.5** — ὀλοιή for ὀλοή, "deadly", only here, 1.342 and Hymn to Aphrodite 224 (Leaf on 22.5).
- **22.6** — Ἰλίου, to be read Ἰλίοο (Leaf on 22.6, referring to his note on 21.104).
- **22.7** — Apollo still has the likeness of Agenor (21.600) (Leaf on 22.7).
- **22.10** — σὺ δέ: the contrast is between the two actions, not the two persons (Leaf on 22.10, comparing 1.191). The second half of the line is 4.32; the Syriac palimpsest adds 4.33 here, out of place (Leaf on 22.10).
- **22.11** — Τρώων πόνος, an objective genitive, "your toil against the Trojans", their slaughter, not their distress; the manuscripts do not have here the usual variant φόνος (Leaf on 22.11).
- **22.13** — μόρσιμος of a person, "predestined", as Od. 16.392 = 21.162; elsewhere only μόρσιμον ἦμαρ and μόρσιμον ἦεν (Leaf on 22.13). Since the speaker is a god, rendered "I am not fated to fall to you".
- **22.15** — ἔβλαψας, "you have foiled me": in this sense used in Homer only of divine agency (9.507, 15.724), except Od. 21.294. Bentley conjectured βλάψας με, ϝεκάεργε; others move μʼ into the next line (Leaf on 22.15). ὀλοώτατε "most baneful", as ὀλοώτερος "more baneful" (3.365) (Leaf on 22.15).
- **22.20** — Compare Od. 2.62; the T scholia report a reading ᾗ for ἦ (Leaf on 22.20).
- **22.23** — For ὅς ῥά τε ῥεῖα the reading ὅς τε ῥεῖα "may be right" (Leaf on 22.23, referring to his note on 17.462); πεδίοιο is most simply taken with θέῃσι, "runs … over the plain" (Leaf on 22.23).
- **22.24** — = 15.269. λαιψηρά is an adverb or a predicative epithet; as an epithet it comes from λαιψηρὰ … γοῦνα (10.358, 20.93; 22.144, 22.204) (Leaf on 22.24).
- **22.26–31** — The star of late summer is Sirius, the ἀστὴρ ὀπωρινός of 5.5, called "the dog of Orion" (29). Sirius is seen at night only in winter and spring, and its rising at dawn in late summer marks the season of fever; Leaf (on 22.27) asks whether the poet joined the star's brightness and deadliness by poetic licence, or whether νυκτὸς ἀμολγῷ ("in the dead of night", as 11.173, 15.324) means the twilight; the same question returns at 317. ἐπίκλησιν, "by the name of", may mean "as a second name", as at 18.487 (Leaf on 22.29). πυρετός, "fever", occurs only here, and in its proper sense (the scholia, as Leaf on 22.31 reports).
- **22.34** — ἀνασχόμενος, with χεῖρας understood, as 23.686: a violent gesture (Leaf on 22.34). Rendered "lifting them up high", the hands of 33.
- **22.41, 22.86** — σχέτλιος, "the hard man!", of Achilles at 41, where the verbs before and after are his; otherwise, Leaf says (on 22.41), the word would more naturally go to Hector, "rash", in the tone of friendly but shocked remonstrance it so often has, and he cites 86 (with 10.164, 18.13). At 86 it is Hecuba's word, and the English takes it there too of Achilles.
- **22.42** — ἔδοιεν, the optative, is Aristarchus' reading; the manuscripts all give ἔδονται. Leaf (on 22.42) prefers the optative, since Priam does not mean to express any confident hope that the dogs will soon eat Achilles.
- **22.43** — κείμενον, "as he lay", unburied; ἀπό goes with ἔλθοι, and ἦ … ἔλθοι is a parenthesis (Leaf on 22.43).
- **22.45** — The T scholia mention a variant θηλυτεράων here too (Leaf on 22.45, comparing 21.454).
- **22.46–55** — Lycaon and Polydorus, Laothoe's sons, die at 21.34–135 and 20.407–418 (Leaf on 22.46). κρείουσα γυναικῶν (48; only here) makes Laothoe a full wife, a sign of polygamy among the Trojans, never hinted at among the Greeks (Leaf on 22.48). Leaf (on 22.51) thinks van Leeuwen right to omit 51 as a late addition, since gifts to a daughter at marriage belong to the end of the Homeric period, and reports that Hoffmann rejected 46–55 and Naber 46–53.
- **22.117–120** — ἄνδιχα πάντα δάσασθαι, "to divide in two all" (120): half of the city's movable goods left to its people, half taken by the besiegers as ransom, the terms put to the besieged city on the shield (18.511), whose sense Leaf (on 18.511) takes from these lines. ἀμφὶς … ἀποδάσσεσθαι (117–118) is rendered "to share out a part of".
- **22.134** — ἀμφί, "around him", to right and left, of the armor generally (Leaf on 22.134, comparing 13.245).
- **22.216** — The digitized text printed νῶι without the diaeresis, the only such spelling in it; νῶϊ is restored.
- **22.254** — θεοὺς ἐπιδώμεθα, "let us call upon the gods": see the note on 10.463.
- **22.261** — ἄλαστε, "accursed one" (for Leaf see his note on 12.163); συνημοσύνας, "agreements", the ἁρμονίαι of 255 (compare συνώμεθα, 13.381), written by Herodian with the rough breathing inside the word (Leaf on 22.261).
- **22.309, 22.362, 22.456** — πεδίον δέ (309, 456) and Ἄϊδος δέ (362) are the suffix of direction printed as a separate word (πεδίονδε, Ἄϊδόσδε), as at 16.856 and 21.3: "down to the plain", "went to Hades", "toward the plain".
- **22.315–316** — = 19.382–383. Leaf (on 22.315) thinks the second line out of place in the old part of the poems, where the making of the arms is unknown; on 19.382 he takes that line as borrowed from here, where Achilles is in rapid motion and περισσείοντο has its full force, and 383 (= 316) as "in both places an interpolation".
- **22.319** — ἀπέλαμπε, impersonal, "there was a gleam", with σέλας or the like understood (19.379), a use hardly paralleled in Homer (Leaf on 22.319). Rendered "so light shone from the well-sharpened point".
- **22.344** — The digitized text printed Ἀχιλλεὺς· with a grave accent before the stop, unlike the same verse at 260 and 1.148; Ἀχιλλεύς· is restored.
- **22.346–347** — Achilles wishes his fury would let him cut Hector's flesh and eat it raw; compare Zeus to Hera, ὠμὸν βεβρώθοις Πρίαμον "and eat Priam raw" (4.35), and Hecuba's wish to eat Achilles' liver (24.212–213).
- **22.356** — προτιόσσομαι: "look at" (Od. 7.31, 23.365) or "forebode" (Od. 5.389, 14.219); rendered "as I look at you".
- **22.358** — = Elpenor's plea to Odysseus (Od. 11.73), the only other place of μήνιμα; the same speech has ἄκλαυτον ἄθαπτον (Od. 11.72; compare 386) and καὶ ἐσσομένοισι πυθέσθαι (Od. 11.76; compare 305).
- **22.359–360** — Achilles' death by Paris and Apollo at the Scaean gates; foretold by the horse Xanthus (19.416–417), and told to Achilles by Thetis (21.277–278).
- **22.389** — εἰ δὲ θανόντων περ καταλήθοντʼ: the subject is not expressed, the dead themselves or men in general; rendered "men forget the dead".
- **22.419** — ἡλικίην: Priam's years, his old age; at 16.808 the word is the men of one's own age ("all of his age"). Rendered "my years".
- **22.437–438** — Ἕκτορος, first in 438 and closed off by the stop, goes with ἄλοχος ("the wife of Hector") or with πέπυστο ("had not yet learned anything of Hector"); the English keeps the Greek order.
- **22.461** — The digitized text ended the line without a stop, though the sentence ends there and 462 begins αὐτὰρ ἐπεί; a period is supplied.
- **22.468–470** — Andromache's headdress: ἄμπυξ "the headband", as of horses (χρυσάμπυκες, 5.358); κεκρύφαλος "the cap" and ἀναδέσμη "the plaited band", both only here in Homer; κρήδεμνον "the veil", Aphrodite's wedding gift (see the note on 16.97–100). Hecuba's καλύπτρη (406) is kept apart as "head covering".
- **22.484–485** — Return as 24.726–727: νήπιος αὔτως "a mere infant", and δυσάμμοροι "most ill-fated" (as 19.315). At 6.400 the same child is νήπιον αὔτως "quite small".
- **22.512–514** — Andromache will burn Hector's clothes "to be your fame": Leaf (on 21.323) sees here, and at Od. 1.291, a hint of a cenotaph, which Homer never mentions.
- **23.7** — ὑπʼ ὄχεσφι, apparently ablatival, "from under the chariots"; it could be taken with ἵππους, "the horses in the chariots", but Leaf (on 23.7) thinks this less likely.
- **23.9** — ὃ γὰρ γέρας ἐστὶ θανόντων: the phrase has τό five times (4.323, 9.422, 16.457, 16.675, Od. 24.296) and ὅ only here and at Od. 24.190; Leaf (on 23.9) finds it "difficult not to believe that ὅ was here meant to be identical with τό". Rendered "for that is the prize of the dead", as 16.457.
- **23.10** — τεταρπώμεσθα γόοιο (again 98), a phrase of Books 23 and 24 and the Odyssey: the word expresses satiety but keeps the sense of satisfaction (Leaf on 23.10). Rendered "taken our fill of deadly lament".
- **23.13** — Here the body lies in the open on the shore, where the Myrmidons and Achilles sleep around it; at 19.211 it lay in the hut (Leaf on 23.13).
- **23.14** — Heyne suspected the line, since Thetis is not among the Myrmidons (Leaf on 23.14).
- **23.19** — καί, "even in Hades, where there is but little rejoicing indeed" (Leaf on 23.19). χαῖρε is rendered "Rejoice" here and at 179 to keep that point; as a greeting among the living it is "Welcome" (9.197) and "Your health" (9.225).
- **23.20** — τελέω is better taken as a present, "already I am fulfilling", an earnest of the whole promise (18.334 ff., 21.27–32, 22.354) (Leaf on 23.20). 180 repeats the line.
- **23.21–23** — ὠμά stands without a noun: either κρέα is understood or the neuter is adverbial (Leaf on 23.21). Payne Knight and others thought 21–23 an interpolation made up from 18.336–337 and Od. 18.87, which Leaf calls "quite possible".
- **23.24** — ἀεικέα … ἔργα: Leaf (on 23.24) thinks they imply no more than leaving the body naked and neglected. The half-line is 22.395, where it is the dragging.
- **23.29** — τάφος in Homer is never the tomb, only the funeral; τάφον δαίνυ as Od. 3.309 (Leaf on 23.29). Rendered "a funeral feast", and τάφου (619), ἐς τάφον (680) "the funeral".
- **23.30** — ἀργοί of oxen "glistening", "sleek" rather than "white" (Leaf on 23.30, after the T scholia, who note that white victims are not offered to the dead and suppose these oxen were for the feast of the living); κύνες ἀργοί keep "swift". ὀρέχθεον, only here in Homer, is obscure; the ancient explanations are "bellowed" (as the knife struck), "stretched themselves out" in death, and "were cut up", and Leaf (on 23.30) thinks the second the most defensible from its etymology, while Theocritus (11.43) took the verb as "roar". Rendered "bellowed".
- **23.34** — κοτυλήρυτον: Aristarchus explained it as so much that it could be drawn up in cups; Leaf (on 23.34) thinks it more natural that the blood was caught in cups and poured out as a gift to the dead, as blood is given to the shades in the Odyssey (Od. 10.535 ff.; 11.89, 11.96, 11.153). Rendered "enough to be drawn in cups".
- **23.43** — ὅς τίς τε with a definite antecedent is almost unique; Monro, quoted by Leaf (on 23.43), took the line as an old formula, "Zeus, or by whatever name the highest of the gods is to be called", comparing Aeschylus, *Agamemnon* 160.
- **23.48** — πειθώμεθα … δαιτί, "let us yield to the hateful feast": Achilles speaks of food as a power that must be obeyed; the variant τερπώμεθα is "quite out of place in his mouth" (Leaf on 23.48). Compare 644–645, γήραϊ λυγρῷ πείθεσθαι.
- **23.50–51** — For ὅσσʼ nearly all manuscripts have ὡς, probably from the line-ending ὡς ἐπιεικές; with ὡς, 51 would have to go (Leaf on 23.50). νέεσθαι is here simply "go".
- **23.62–65** — The sentence begins at 62, and its main clause at 65, "there came to him the soul" (Leaf on 23.62). Ἕκτορʼ (64) is the dative with ἐπαΐσσων, "rushing at Hector … toward Ilios" (Leaf on 23.64).
- **23.71–74** — Patroclus cannot enter Hades until he is buried: the souls keep him beyond the river.
